I ask the Secretary of State in the Ministry of Justice of Minister Michael Wójcik to present the justification for the bill. You're welcome, Minister.
ParlaMint-PL_2016-07-07-sejm-22-3.u461	Dear Marshal, Ladies and gentlemen! The main objective of today's draft law amending the Civil Procedure Code and some other laws is to extend the scope of the Land Register to European Certificate of Succession. Let me start by discussing in a few sentences what the Fall Registry is. It was established in July 2015 by the Act amending the Civil Code Act and the Code of Civil Procedure Act and some other laws and will be operational from September 2015. It's gonna be the day this bill comes into force. It will be a public register kept by the National Notarial Council, which is used to disclose information on judgments and documents issued by notaries confirming the rights of inheritance. The European Certificate of Succession was introduced by a Regulation of the European Parliament and of the Council of 2012 on jurisdiction, applicable law, recognition and enforcement of decisions, acceptance and enforcement of authentic instruments relating to succession and on the establishment of a European Certificate of Succession, as well as the Act of July 2015 amending the Code of Civil Procedure Act, the Law on notariat and certain other laws and functioning since 17 August 2015. It can be said that this is another, in addition to the court's order to establish the acquisition of the inheritance and the certificate of inheritance issued by notaries, and which may be an alternative to these documents, a document for the exercise of rights to the inheritance. It may be issued by a court or notary, depending on the choice of the person concerned, and its main advantage is that it produces effects in all the Member States of the European Union bound by the Regulation, without the need for separate recognition or declaration of enforceability. It should be noted that the Revenue Register in its original form could not include European Certificate of Succession, because at the time of finalising its work on this law, the legislative work on the Law amending the Code of Civil Procedure Act, the Law on the Notariat and some other laws aimed at implementing the regulations already mentioned by me were in progress. The proposed law is intended to implement what was not possible at the time of the adoption of the original rules on the Land Register and to complete the process of adopting the national provisions implementing the Regulation. I would like to stress that, in the view of the government, extending the Land Register to European inheritance certificates is a good step, it was desirable and expected. First of all, registration will increase the effectiveness of the European Certificate of Succession itself, and this will contribute to the effective implementation of the Regulation. The inclusion of the Land Register also of European inheritance certificates will increase the credibility of the Land Register as a whole. Strengthen legal certainty and minimise the risk of issuing conflicting decisions or notarial documents confirming the rights of inheritance. For these reasons, in the proposed law, we propose amendments to the Civil Procedure Code, the Law on notaries and the above-mentioned Act of July 2015, which will enable the registration of judgments and notarial acts concerning European inheritance certificates in the Land Register. Such registration shall be carried out by an official court or notary, depending on who issued the document to be entered in the register. In the Act we propose a few additional changes. These amendments are of a more precise, corrective or adaptive nature. They refer to the provisions relating to the drawing up of acts of inheritance certificate, the keeping of the Land Register, as well as the procedure for notaries to submit applications for entry in the perpetual register and the fees for entries made in the Land Register. In accordance with the draft law, the law should enter into force on 8 September 2016. In this way, the European Certificate of Succession will be covered by the Revenue Register from the very beginning of its operation. Thank you very much, Marshal.

ParlaMint-PL_2016-07-07-sejm-22-3.u471	Mr. Marshal! High Izbo! I would like to present to you a draft law amending the Agricultural Chambers Act. The amended law dates back to 1995, i.e. more than 20 years old. 20 years, which for Polish agriculture were a period of dynamic changes, intensive modernisation and restructuring of agricultural holdings and huge changes in the Polish countryside. Agricultural chambers were not a small part of these changes. Unfortunately, at many meetings, farmers now report that agricultural chambers do not function as they should, that chambers do not work for farmers, do not represent the interests of agriculture. It is evident that the agricultural government is not keeping pace with the pace of changes that are taking place in agriculture, and that is why a reform is needed which will adapt the ossified institution of agricultural chambers to the current agricultural situation in Poland, Europe and the world. I would like to emphasise and remind you that agricultural self-government is an organ that has a rich, more than 120-year-old tradition. The history of agricultural chambers on Polish lands begins in Poznań in 1895, then still belonging to the Prussian partition. The task of the then chambers was to cooperate with government administration, to improve economic law, to represent and defend the interests of agriculture and forestry, to establish and run agricultural schools, and to support technological progress in agricultural production. The interwar period brought about a boom in the agricultural chambers. At that time, there were 13 agricultural chambers throughout the country. The regulations of President Ignacy Mościcki of 1928 and of 1932 defined the institution of agricultural chambers, defined their essence and defined a fairly wide range of competences. Already back then, agricultural chambers had a compulsory membership, and their universal character ensured that they could represent the interests of all farmers. The main objective of the agricultural chambers was also underlined: organisation of the agricultural profession and cooperation in the agricultural field. The agricultural government actively implemented agricultural progress in the countryside, in all its possible forms, thereby raising the prestige and rank of the farmer's profession. The active activities of agricultural chambers interrupted the outbreak of World War II. After the war, the new power significantly hindered the operation of the chambers until they were completely liquidated by the Decree of the Council of Ministers of 26 August 1946 signed by the President of the National Council of Bolesław Bierut, President of the Council of Ministers Władysław Gomułka and the then Minister of Agriculture Stanisław Bieńk. Only after almost 50 years of non-existence was the Agricultural Government appointed by the Law of 14 December 1995 by the Sejm II of its term of office during the SLD-PSL coalition, which then had 33 mandates. At this point it is worth looking at the model of agricultural self-government in other countries, analyze its functioning and then compare with Polish solutions. In terms of functioning, the model for the Polish Chambers should be the French Chambers, which, thanks to strong legal bases, have a very strong impact on the creation of agricultural policy. An important feature of the agricultural government in France is its relationship with trade unions. Agricultural chambers and trade unions work together because they have the same objective: to protect the interests of the farmer. The elections to the agricultural chambers enjoy a very large turnout of up to 90%. which allows chambers to have a strong mandate, with which both state and local authorities and private entrepreneurs and foreign corporations count. The agricultural government in France is able to defend the interests of agriculture firmly and, most importantly, the actions it takes are very effective. Polish agricultural chambers often put the model of the French self-government as a model to which we should strive. They mention one obstacle, which is explained by their powerlessness, powerlessness, passivity, namely the smaller budget of Polish agricultural chambers than their French counterparts. However, it must be realised that only 1/2 of the budget of the French agricultural government comes from taxes, while 1/4 are funds from various types of contracts, projects, and the remainder are revenues from the provision of commercial services. Thus, half of the budget of the chambers depends on their entrepreneurship, commitment and activity in raising funds. In Germany, the chambers deal with e.g. promotion of entrepreneurship, management of agricultural areas, advice. They also carry out the tasks commissioned by the Lands, in their duties they also have training, consultation and laboratory tests. It should be stressed that the German agricultural government is not involved in politics, but focuses on actual aid to farmers in their daily problems, looking for ways to increase income from farming. In my opinion, the ideal model for agricultural chambers in Poland would therefore be to combine the experience of French and German chambers. On the one hand, a strong, influential and vibrant government, courageously defending the interests of agriculture, on the other hand, a local government which acts as close as possible to farmers, at local level, which supports them, enjoys their trust and broad support. Today, agricultural chambers do not represent farmers. This is demonstrated by a very low turnout in the elections. In 2015, according to the National Council of Agricultural Chambers it amounted to only 4.34%, with as many as six voivodships being less than 3%: in the Mazowieckie Voivodeship - 2.79, Opole - 2.41, Podkarpackie - 2.87, Podlaski - 2.74, Silesian - 2.79 In the Lesser Poland Voivodeship it was only 1.86%. This means that there is virtually no interest in the candidates themselves and the elections. Farmers don't feel like their voice is gonna change anything. Thus, the slogan of the elections: The agricultural institutions unite all farmers is very out of date. The causes of low attendance should be seen in several different factors. This is primarily a low awareness of farmers about the very existence of chambers, the lack of information about their activities, their role, their tasks. On the other hand, it can be argued that the lack of awareness of their existence is due to the fact that the activities of chambers are currently marginal. Farmers lack the sense that anybody represents their interests, and yet such an organ should be chambers. Today, it seems that agricultural chambers represent only a small group of farmers who choose their members, which is less than 4%. eligible. If chambers had more support, if their mandate had been based on solid foundations, it would have been the power received from most farmers, farmers, farmers, owners of farms, then perhaps they would have been more courageous and more effective at protecting the interests of the Polish countryside and agriculture. There was another problem at this point, namely insufficient information on the place and time of the elections. They are known from the press and from the farmers' stories of cases in which the wife of one of the candidates sat on the electoral committee, or cases of rejection of persons entitled to vote by the committee, even though they met all the conditions required. Another reprehensible behavior was the use of mobile, escaping urns. Mechanisms are needed to mobilise chambers to work in the electoral campaign, which will ensure better information about the upcoming elections and about candidates' silhouettes, so that elections are in line with democratic principles and not merely an appearance of democracy. Such mechanisms are included in our bill. It clarifies where, when and when elections are to take place, as well as detailed rules for the conduct of the election campaign and information about elections. The consequence of the proposed amendments will be that information on elections will reach a significant proportion of the members of the Chamber. The awareness of membership in the farmhouse will also increase and thus the involvement in the work of the chambers will increase. As a result, this will translate into a much greater turnout in elections, stronger grounds for action and an increase in the importance and reconstruction of agricultural self-government. Another reason for the weaknesses of the chambers is that farmers do not identify themselves with the agricultural authorities, because in their opinion, in addition to the role of opinion-girls, they do not have the right powers to be able to really influence the development of agricultural policy. However, the fact that chambers do not carry out their tasks does not mean that they do not have them. The Agricultural Chambers Act lists 18 tasks belonging to the Chambers, including: drawing up analyses, evaluations, opinions, applications for agricultural production and the agricultural market and presenting them to government and local authorities, advising on agricultural activities, undertaking measures for the development of agricultural and rural infrastructure and improving the agro-structure, promoting export of agricultural products or developing cooperation with foreign agricultural producer organisations. In addition, the powers of the chambers have been defined, inter alia. in the Law on the Protection of Agricultural Lands and Forests, in the Law on Seeding, on Plant Protection, in the Law on Agricultural Advisory Units and several other laws. I therefore believe that not too little competence, but infirmity, inactivity and passiveness are currently behind the weakness and low position of the agricultural chambers. Perhaps this is due to the formation of the institutions of the agricultural self-government, the function of the same persons, and perhaps too politicising chambers, which have become a representative not of farmers but of party interests. Agricultural chambers should focus more on rural management, the promotion of rural entrepreneurship, agricultural advice and not on political issues. It cannot be said that everything is fine when farmers and statistics confirm that changes are necessary. The current stagnation is no good for anyone. Our bill is an attempt to take up the subject, an impulse to discuss, a broad debate on the reform of agricultural self-government, on its future shape. The project primarily cleans up election issues, but also funding issues, so that chambers become an effective, dynamic body, creatively undertaking various initiatives, dynamically and actively acting in favour of farmers. The current law provides for the financing of agricultural chambers with a 2% write-off for agricultural tax paid by farmers. Our project provides that agricultural chambers will be provided with 1% tax and the remainder, i.e. 1% the farmer will be able to decide for himself and may transfer him to the activities of an organisation which will represent his interests - an agricultural trade union or a farmhouse. Thus, with proper involvement in the interests of farmers, the chambers will continue to receive 2%. The proposed regulation will contribute to raising awareness of the existence and operation of chambers, and will mobilise chambers to work hard for farmers, so that chambers will be anxious to prove to members the need for their existence. An important aspect is the fact that farmers are subject to evaluation of the activities of chambers, which will ensure that they feel the real impact on the functioning and performance of tasks by the agricultural authorities. This will be an important stimulus, an impetus that will mobilise chambers to act effectively in favour of farmers. The House will become a real representative of the interests of its members and will provide an interest in agriculture and the countryside not only before the elections but also during the entire term of office. During the public consultation, a number of new opinions on the proposed law appeared. We respect all evaluations and, after careful consideration of the comments, we decided to change some of the provisions of the project in the self-correction mode. An important issue for us was the elimination of the reservations formulated by the Sejm Analysis Office, according to which the possibility to transfer 1% the agricultural tax in the form proposed in the draft law may constitute unlawful State aid within the meaning of Article. 107 Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union. The proposed amendment aims to clarify the provisions on the transferability of 1% agricultural tax for agricultural chambers, socio-occupational farmers' organisations and trade unions of individual farmers, in the light of which the funds received from this source can only be used for the pursuit of public benefit activities. We are also making changes on requirements to support the election of the Chambers. The candidate may submit at least 10 farmers of the holding concerned and, in a county where the population is less than 50 persons, the application will require at least 10% support. farmers. In this way, a lower support threshold is introduced in communities where the population is low. It was very important for us to find in the draft a provision which is not in the present law, namely allowing a member of the general meeting to be dismissed by the county council of the Chamber. The appeal of a member of the General Assembly in accordance with the self-correction shall be made at the request of at least 10% the members of the county council who have chosen the member concerned. The earlier requirement for an appeal to be supported by at least 100 inhabitants of the village concerned does not take account of the different size of the village. It could happen that in small villages with fewer than 100 inhabitants, the procedure for appeal of a member of the Chamber would be impossible at all. The determination of the percentage of the required number of inhabitants of the county is a fair solution and reflects the bottom-up character of the chambers. Furthermore, in order to implement democratic principles, we want to introduce the right to submit an electoral protest, which was very lacking in the present law. An entity entitled to submit an electoral protest will be given the opportunity if its protest is not taken into account by the National Council of Agricultural Chambers to submit a general electoral complaint to the court. This institution will prevent abuse and irregularities and will contribute to the principle of impartiality and objectivity in the conduct of elections. In addition, it will ensure internal election control and will have a preventive role. The bodies responsible for the conduct of the elections, knowing that their activities can be controlled, will be more careful to ensure that they are properly carried out. In conclusion, I would like to emphasise once again that this project will be the starting point for the debate on the different agricultural environments on the future of agricultural chambers, the future shape of the law. We do not insist that the solutions we propose are the only appropriate ones. We want everyone to be able to express their views and to develop optimal regulations. Remember, too, that only constructive criticism is valuable. The purpose of the amendment of the Agricultural Chambers Act has already been raised several times by parliamentarians themselves as well as by members of the Agricultural Chambers. To this day, despite many work and various proposals for a new law, nothing has been done. I hope that a wide range of people from different backgrounds will join in the drafting of the bill and that we will be able to work out, create a law that will contribute to the importance of agricultural chambers, so that farmers will gain real representation. Thank you. [[Applause]]

ParlaMint-PL_2016-07-07-sejm-22-3.u482	Thank you very much, sir. Please take the floor of Mr Ireneus Gain.
ParlaMint-PL_2016-07-07-sejm-22-3.u483	Mr. Marshal! High Seym! On behalf of the Freedom and Solidarnia Circle, I present the position of the circle on the draft law amending the Act on Agricultural Chambers and certain other laws. The applicants in the explanatory memorandum indicate that the purpose of the bill is to introduce new rules for holding elections to the General Assembly of Agricultural Chambers and to introduce new rules for raising funds for the implementation of the statutory and statutory tasks and objectives of the Agricultural Chambers. They stress the weakness of the functioning of the agricultural government due to the low awareness of farmers about their existence, role or tasks. The Circle of Free and Solidarni as supporting efforts to strengthen civil society and its various forms of organisation, including the proper and independent of the government of the functioning of professional self-governments, welcomes the intentions behind the project. It should be noted, however, that many proposals for amendments to the law have been subjected to constructive criticism by the organisations that are opinionating the project, in particular by the Polish County Union. I'll mention some of the most important things. Firstly, the amendment provides that passive electoral law, the right of election, has a member of the farmhouse who has been resident in the area of the town where he is running for at least 3 years. Such a provision will result in any change of residence having the effect of losing an active electoral right for a period of 3 years, even if the person concerned is still a member of the same Chamber. The place of residence within the same municipality is changed e.g. for the purpose of marriage, this does not affect the pursuit of agricultural activities. Secondly, the descent to the level of the Councils when it comes to holding elections to the bodies of the Agricultural Chambers. Although it is a noble idea and intended to strengthen capacity, it may pose serious organisational problems, mainly due to the huge diversity of the area and the number of inhabitants of the villages. The smallest number of inhabitants is about 40 and 50, some of them the largest, several thousand each. This has consequences for other amended regulations. In the smallest villages, it will be difficult to meet the criterion of support for a candidate for a general meeting by at least 10 farmers living in a given village. For there are villages in which less than 10 farmers live. I know that the applicants will make an autocorrection in this regard. This is a good solution to this problem, but I would like to point out that there are also rural areas that have been incorporated into urban communities. I myself live in a village that used to be a separate village, but is now included in the municipality, typically urban, not even urban-rural, but the municipality. The only such celebration, which shows that these rural traditions are continued, are the communal harvests organized under the aegis of the local parish. In this situation, there would be no possibility that there would be elections to the agricultural chambers in our village. This definitely requires another amendment. Although I understand the intention of introducing regulations governing the election campaign in elections to chambers, it is debatable to introduce an obligation to place electoral announcements in specific locations in the form of a closed catalogue, i.e. on municipal information boards, in shops, at stops and on local sites and on websites. Would not the failure to put an announcement in one of these places be a violation of a law that could be an argument to challenge the results of the vote? It is also debatable to require the Chief to hold elections within the Council, looking at its position as a body. The Council does not have an official apparatus or financial means to enable it to carry out its public tasks on its own. It should be added that the establishment of the municipality itself is optional. It therefore appears that the applicants have too hastily abandoned the current formula which makes the number of candidates selected in chambers dependent on the agricultural area and the number of persons residing in the area concerned. However, the Free and Solidarnia Circle welcomes this legislative initiative. We therefore request that the draft law be referred to the Sejm Committee for further work. Thank you very much. We proceed with the examination of item 30 of the agenda: Reports on the activities of the General Data Protection Officer in 2014 and 2015 (prints Nos 116 and 582) together with the position of the Committee on Justice and Human Rights (print No 619). Please take the floor of the General Data Protection Officer, Mrs Edyta Bielak-Jomaa. You're welcome.
ParlaMint-PL_2016-07-07-sejm-22-3.u503	Thank you very much. Mrs. Marshal! High Izbo! I have the honour to present annual reports on the activities of the General Data Protection Officer for 2014 and 2015 together with the conclusions resulting from the assessment of compliance with the provisions on personal data protection. Given my position as General Data Protection Officer in April 2015, presenting the results of the body's work for 2 reporting years, including 2014 I also present a report for the period when my predecessor, Dr. Wojciech Rafał Wiewiórowski, was in office. The report shall be the implementation of the Article. 20 of the Personal Data Protection Act of 29 August 1997 pursuant to which the General Data Protection Officer submits to the Sejm once a year a report on his activity together with the conclusions resulting from compliance with the provisions on personal data protection. This obligation arises from the implementation into Polish law of art. Article 28 (2) (a) and (b) of Regulation (EU) No 1303/2013 5 Directive of the European Parliament and of the Council of 24 October 1995 on the protection of individuals with regard to the processing of personal data and on the free movement of such data, according to which each supervisory authority is required to draw up a report on its activities and make it public. The General Data Protection Officer is a single-person public administration body dealing with issues having its constitutional legitimacy, because both the right to privacy and the right to the protection of personal data have been explicitly guaranteed in Chapter 2 of the Constitution of the Republic of Poland concerning human rights and freedoms and citizens. First of all, I would like to stress that it is up to the very many tasks of the General Data Protection Officer to monitor compliance with the provisions on personal data protection, to keep a register of personal data collections and records of information security administrators, to deal with complaints and to issue administrative decisions, to ensure the performance of obligations of a non-monetary nature resulting from decisions of the authority, by applying enforcement measures provided for in the Enforcement Act in the Administration, to evaluate draft laws and regulations on the protection of personal data and to disseminate information on the protection of personal data, to cooperate internationally with organisations and institutions that deal with this issue. The General Inspector shall also carry out tasks which are provided for in specific provisions, e.g. the provisions of telecommunications law on the basis of which the data protection authority accepts from providers of publicly available telecommunications services a notification of a breach of the security of personal data. A similar task was imposed on GIODO following the entry into force of the eIDAS Regulation a few days ago. The General Inspector shall be responsible for the implementation and supervision of data protection legislation and shall therefore cooperate with different actors in both the country and abroad, including data protection authorities from and outside Europe, as well as institutions dealing with the wider protection of human rights, in addition to sovereign activities. As of 1 January 2015, the revised provisions of the Personal Data Protection Act began to apply. The changes concerned the status, tasks and functioning of an information security administrator whose appointment by data controllers has become voluntary but at the same time the role of ABI has increased. In addition, new rules on the transfer of personal data to third countries have been introduced. Thus, the obligation to obtain GIODO's consent for the transfer of personal data to a third country which does not ensure an adequate level of protection of personal data in its territory has been waived if it is transferred on the basis of standard contractual clauses already approved by the European Commission or binding corporate rules. The solutions adopted, regardless of their purpose of deregulation, were also intended to prepare data controllers for the so-called rules. the general data protection regulation as part of the package of reforms planned in the European Union in the field of personal data protection. After many years of intensive work on modernising European data protection law in December 2015, the texts of new legislation regulating data protection in the European Union were accepted at political level. They were finally adopted in April 2016. 25 May of this year entered into force, directly in force since 25 May 2018, a general EU data protection regulation and the so-called "EU Data Protection Regulation." the police directive on the protection of personal data. The Regulation, which is directly applicable in the territory of the Member States, unifies the rules on data protection in all Member States of the European Union. It is worth noting that representatives of the Polish Parliament were also actively involved in the discussions conducted at both European and national level. We are currently facing legislative challenges related to the implementation of both legislative acts. The General Data Protection Officer, with regard to his competence and tasks, as I have already mentioned, maintains two national public registers: the Register of Information Security Administrators and the Register of Personal Data Collections. The former is carried out following changes which entered into force on 1 January 2015. Administrators of information security, whose appointment is not mandatory, in the light of the new rules, guarantee the real protection of the personal data of Polish citizens. Such a definition of the role of information security administrators has started the process of creating a group of professionals, thanks to which the actual level of protection of personal data will be higher than before. Despite the transitional period and the relatively late adoption of parts of the implementing acts to the Act, the interest in setting up a new information security administrator exceeded the wildest expectations. In 2015, approximately 23 ABI applications were received by the office, of which 2/3 were entered in the register. Unfortunately, despite a fairly simple, clearly defined legal form of notification of the appointment of ABI 1/3 of the submitted applications, there was a lack of formal or other errors. The second of the registers kept by the General Inspector since the beginning of the Data Protection Act is the National Open Register of Personal Data Collections. The primary purpose of the obligation to register data sets was to ensure the transparency of personal data processing operations and, to varying degrees, the initial checks of such operations. In 2014, administrators reported more than 43 000 to the General Inspector for registration. collections and in 2015 more than 31.5 thousand data sets. Annual experience with the application of the new provisions of the Act shows the progressive decline in the importance of registering personal data sets and the increasing role of information security administrators. Another task pursued by the General Data Protection Officer is to deal with complaints. The number of complaints affecting GIODO in 2014 and 2015 remains around 2.5 thousand. per year. Complaints are dealt with when all the rigors of the Code of Administrative Procedure are met, and I would like to stress this very strongly. There is an increase in complaints in the public administration sector and in the health sector. In the case of public administration, the complaint most frequently concerned the inclusion of non-anonymised documents by local authorities in the Public Information Bulletin, such as protocols or resolutions that contained personal data. On the other hand, complaints relating to health care activities were most often related to the non-security of medical records and access to unauthorised persons. Despite a decrease in the number of complaints concerning the processing of personal data on the Internet, the marketing sector and public security, this number is still very high. Invariably for years, complaints against entities operating in the marketing sector concern the processing of personal data for marketing purposes without the consent of the data subject or despite his opposition to the processing of data for marketing purposes. With regard to the telecommunications sector, we continue to note cases of unauthorised refusals by telecom operators to make available to authorised entities the information necessary to conduct their own proceedings. In such a situation she found herself, for example. an urban guard who could not initiate and conduct infringement proceedings, as required by the law, in view of the refusal to provide data on the perpetrators of offences by telecommunications network operators. The number of these complaints in the telecommunications sector is high, remains at more than 130 and in one, and in the second reporting year. As in previous years, also in 2014 and 2015 there were complaints alleging the processing of personal data by parish priests of the Catholic Church. As a result of complaints by the parties to the proceedings against the decision of GIODO, both the complainants and the parish priests, the administrative courts presented an uneven judicial line. Initially, in the assessment of the Supreme Administrative Court, the inspector-general should have established the issue of updating the personal data of apostates on the basis of generally applicable rules, while according to the current position, the inspector-general should settle these matters in accordance with the internal rules of the Church. Despite the lack of an increase in the number of complaints against banks and other financial institutions, this number is still worryingly high. It should be stressed that these complaints most frequently questioned the legality of the processing of data in respect of the no longer applicable credit card agreement and the failure of the bank to comply with the information obligation. There are also repeated complaints about banks processing incorrect personal data and refusing to correct them in order to correct the spelling of the name. Banks often refer to internal rules, which cannot be contrary to common law. Thus, even if the bank has reserved in its rules of procedure that in the case of payment cards it has the right to process the personal data of its clients without Polish marks, in the opinion of the authority, such a solution is incorrect because it is not possible to impose such a solution on the customer in general. The customer should have the right not to consent to this or the right to request that his data be corrected. It should be borne in mind that the controller of personal data is obliged to use such technical solutions that allow the processing of factually correct data. This does not justify the fact that the bank's IT system was so designed that it did not take into account the use of foreign diacritics. Another task of the General Data Protection Officer is to control the processing of data. The inspection activities carried out by the inspectors of the GIODO Bureau were sectoral inspections and ad hoc inspections. In 2015, sectoral controls included, for example, entities that carried out tasks under the Large Family Charter Act, entities holding personal and wage records, medicinal entities, data processing bodies in Eurodac, bodies having access to the Schengen Information System and the Visa Information System, sports equipment rentals and audio guides. The selection of sectors subject to inspections was based on an analysis of signals reported to GIODO, an analysis of complaints and reports of personal data sets for registration, and was the result of international obligations. On the other hand, issues covered by ad hoc checks were closely related to the handling of complaints or requests by other bodies. During the reporting years, inspectors carried out a total of 350 inspections, including 13 checks commissioned to security administrators. Checking is a new instrument provided for by the Data Protection Act. It should be noted that the number of checks carried out has a significant impact on the complexity of the processing of personal data which is subject to control. The controls were carried out in entities with a very different organisational structure, size or very different technological solutions. It is important for the efficiency of the control of individuals using information systems that they have a multi-stage and very complex functionality. It is therefore important that inspectors have both the appropriate level of substantive knowledge in the field of legislation and, of course, new technologies, but also the ability to carry out control activities. It is therefore increasingly necessary to increase the scope of activities during the preparatory phase of the audit. In connection with the information provided to the General Data Protection Officer by individuals or by GIODO's controls during the reporting period, he sent a notice to the authorities appointed to prosecute the offences 34 that the persons responsible for processing had suspected the offence. The basis was the violation of one of the criminal provisions of the Data Protection Act, in particular Article. 49 - processing of personal data without a legal basis, Article. 51 - sharing of personal data with unauthorised persons, Article. 52 - breach of the data protection obligation, Article. 54a - Criminal liability shall be borne by the person who makes it impossible to carry out the checks. With regard to the notifications sent by GIODO to law enforcement authorities, it should be pointed out that in nine cases an investigation was initiated, six were terminated, seven were refused to initiate an investigation, in other cases, because of the lack of information from law enforcement authorities, the general inspector is not aware of the further fate of those notifications. On the basis of the article. 19a The Data Protection Act may be addressed by the General Data Protection Officer to public authorities, local government authorities, public and municipal organisational units, non-public entities carrying out public tasks, natural and legal persons, organisational units not legal persons and other entities seeking to ensure effective protection of personal data. It may also request the competent authorities to take a legislative initiative or to issue or amend legal acts in matters relating to the protection of personal data. The requested entity shall be required to respond to that request or request in writing within 30 days of its receipt. In 2014 the Council GIODO gave 66 speeches, and in 2015 they were already 80. GIODO's requests to the competent authorities for a legislative initiative or for the issue or amendment of legal acts were based both on legal gaps in the protection of privacy and respect for the right of the entity to protect its personal data, as well as on inaccuracies in existing and newly created legal regulations. Such a situation has resulted in an inconsistent practice of the data controllers, forcing the response of the data protection authority. In GIODO's speeches, he stressed a noticeable tendency to collect more and more information about citizens by both private and public actors, at the same time pointing out that Article. The Commission shall adopt implementing acts laying down detailed rules for the implementation of this Regulation. The 2nd Constitution explicitly states that public authority can only collect information about a citizen that is necessary in a democratic legal state. Example: In a speech to the mayor of the city concerning the collection of detailed personal data in surveys given to visitors to the city museum, we indicated that the survey should be anonymous. There is no reason to collect back-up information that can be useful, useful, necessary, logically or economically viable. The quality of the law created is important for data protection, and it is therefore vital to take account of personal data protection issues at the stage of drafting legislative projects. From the perspective of the data protection authority, it is extremely important to be able to comment at the initial stages of the legislative process on the compatibility of draft legal acts with the data protection rules, because this allows for avoiding errors and for developing legislative solutions which, when pursuing the objectives of the projector, would comply with the principles of the protection of personal data. As regards matters relating to legislative action, the Data Protection Authority is still looking forward to working on the draft guidelines of the Video Monitoring Act, which will properly guarantee the constitutional freedoms and rights of the individual. The proposals so far have not sufficiently defined the powers of the observed persons, the designation of the monitored spaces, the extent to which surveillance recordings are made available. More widely referring to the various legislative proposals of GIODO, it should be noted that my office supports the efforts of the public administration to improve the management of their systems and their personal data, as well as to facilitate citizens' access to the so-called ones. e-services in administration. However, these solutions should take into account the appropriate level and guarantee of the protection of personal data. Ensuring such guarantees, as I am convinced, is an essential condition for building citizens' confidence in these services. Therefore, at the drafting stage, the General Inspector calls in his opinions for the projectors to take due account of alternatives to the proposed ones, which are more favourable to the protection of personal data, i.e. those which do not assume the use of data in excess. Computerisation of growing information resources may lead to new threats to privacy and data protection, such as the provision of data to unauthorised persons, loss or theft of data, but also the collection of specific types of personal data where only statistical data or identity theft are sufficient. Notwithstanding the development of new technologies for the processing of personal data, the body appointed to protect them shall stand and uphold legitimate, including constitutional principles, and adequate to the purposes of the processing of personal data, to carry out limited operations within a period of time, and shall ensure that the interpretation of the applicable law is unambiguous. After examining the projects that were received by the GIODO Office in 2014 and 2015, it should be noted that there are still proposals for collecting unnecessary, inadequate, excessive personal data in relation to what is necessary to achieve the objectives of the proposed solutions. Many of these solutions do not pass the constitutional test, constituting an excessive interference with the right to data protection, which is also not sufficiently justified in breach of the criterion of necessity and proportionality. I would like to point out that interference with the right to data protection requires a statutory act. Unfortunately, designers often try to regulate these issues in implementing acts rather than in the law. This is particularly serious with regard to the processing of sensitive data referred to in Article. 27 Data Protection Act, because their processing requires a statutory basis which introduces adequate safeguards for their processing. It is also inappropriate to process or exchange information on the basis of cooperation agreements or agreements between public authorities where these issues also require a statutory basis. It should be noted that there are still situations where the designer does not send draft legal acts for the opinion of the general inspector, which in practice means that the rules adopted may be incompatible with the rules on the protection of personal data. In 2014 the Council GIODO gave an opinion on 601 draft legal acts and in 2015 on 703 projects. A permanent element of GIODO's activities is to answer questions about the interpretation of personal data protection regulations. For the last 4 years, around 4,5,000 have been coming to GIODO's office annually. questions about interpretation of the rules. The thematic scope of these questions remained very wide, concerned many aspects of data processing, addressed a very wide range of private and public actors that process data. I think it is worth pointing out the issues that are still valid, in particular those relating to the administration and the judiciary. Here the questions concerned and concern issues relating to public disclosure, including on websites, of a personal nature. Many doubts have been raised in connection with the acquisition of personal data without a legal basis or to a greater extent than provided for by law, as well as access to public information in the judicial field. The questions concerned the disclosure of court vocals. Another sector from which we receive a lot of legal questions is education. Questions relate to monitoring the fate of graduates, access to documentation by the school supervisor. Financial institutions. Here were doubts and requests to resolve doubts about disclosure, e.g. by the financial supervisory authorities, information on the reports of suspected offences, the implementation of FATCA legislation or the making available of the history of the deceased client's bank account, as well as the exchange of consumer information by creditors to assess credit conformity. The questions concerned the processing by BIK of personal data from publicly available, open registers, including: KRS or REGON. The health sector. There have been doubts as to the basis for the processing of health data, the provision of data by medicinal products to other entities, e.g. The police or the ZUS. The problem of the employment sector. Questions relating to the permissible scope of data and the reasons for legalising the use of data by employers, including the transfer of employee data to third parties or the use of biometric data to control working time. Invariably, the housing sector. Here it is absolutely possible to see the repetition of certain issues. For years these entities have been asking us questions. The questions concern the legality of making available information on the remuneration of persons managing cooperatives and communities, the rights of tenants, the access to contracts, the posting of information concerning the debt of members of the cooperative on notice boards on staircases or entrance doors. Telecommunications, Internet and monitoring. Here, the lack of regulation causes us to get a lot of questions about the use of recording devices by both individuals and various institutions. These examples remain only part of a very wide range of issues that are addressed in GIODO's letters. I would like to point out that, due to the burden on workers, we are increasingly trying to prepare more general information material that could be useful for a larger group of people on the basis of the questions that are coming. The effective activities of the data protection authority must not be limited to the exercise of the law. It is also necessary to raise awareness of both data subjects and data controllers. An educational action in the field of personal data protection shall be carried out by the general inspector through cooperation with entities from many different sectors, government institutions, schools, universities, chambers of crafts, representatives of professional self-governments and judicial authorities. We've been doing a lot of programs for a few years, but there's one thing I'd like to pay particular attention to. It is an educational programme aimed at schools called: Your name is your business. Each year, more than 200 educational institutions participate in it on average. On the basis of the experience of implementing the programme, the huge involvement of teachers, students and parents, we initiated an international project which will result in the preparation of a manual for teachers on the protection of privacy and personal data. A very important part of the activities of the General Data Protection Officer was working internationally to create a new legal framework for the protection of personal data in the European Union. GIODO participates both in the work of the Article 29 Working Party. The protection of data of data subjects in all EU countries, as well as in the work of the Council of Europe. Thanks to its cooperation with the Council of Ministers, GIODO also has the opportunity to attend meetings of the Council of the European Union Working Group as an expert accompanying the government delegation and providing its substantive support, which was linked to the work on the reform of personal data protection in the European Union. For me and for my predecessors, cooperation with data protection authorities of the Visegrad Group and the Eastern Partnership is an extremely important part of international activity. Since 2006, the General Inspector has been actively involved in the work of the Central and Eastern European Group of States and supports the knowledge and experience of the newly created data protection authorities, e.g. Georgia, Moldova or Macedonia. For example, in 2015, we hosted employees of the Croatian data protection authority and then representatives of the Georgian authority, with whom we shared our experience in the protection of personal data. Mrs. Marshal! High Izbo! Among the challenges facing the general inspector in the near future, it should be pointed out first and foremost that there are a number of issues relating to the development of modern technologies that still need analysis and regulation, including, among others. The issue of identification and authentication in public administration electronics systems, access to location data, mobile applications, protection of individual rights in connection with regulation of drones, identification and analysis of risks arising from the dissemination of biometric solutions in citizen's relations with public and private institutions, and unfortunately the increasing risks associated with identity theft. Another challenge facing us is the challenge of data processing in a very dynamic Internet of things, the threat or the challenge of ensuring effective protection of the privacy of users of devices connected to the network. As a data protection officer, I encourage the development of technologies that will introduce new ways of protecting personal data and consumer privacy from the very beginning, i.e. from the moment of design of the service or product. The protection of personal data in the design phase and the default protection of personal data should no longer be considered as something peculiar or merely an idea, but should become a key feature of innovative technologies. As a result of technological changes in information processing or new business models that use personal data, the tasks of the data protection authority are naturally increased so that the authority can respond adequately to new challenges and effectively protect citizens' rights. This is reflected in the imposition on the general inspector of new competences, which are linked to new threats. For a few days now, GIODO under the eIDAS Regulation has been competent to receive from trust service providers reports of a personal data breach. Further tasks and competences are introduced by the General Data Protection Regulation. This will be among others. General acceptance of notifications of data breaches by all data controllers, consultation of data controllers when they intend to conduct so-called. risky operations. In addition, the new rules will require a number of actions of an international nature, to an extent that is incomparable to the current situation. I would like to stress that all these challenges require GIODO to ensure that its staff have adequate legal and technological knowledge and skills that require continuous improvement and development. All the activities I've been talking about here are done by the general inspector with 145 employees. The budget of the office is PLN 16 million. This is still very little taking into account the fact that GIODO's actions are nationwide and, once European Union rules have been harmonised, cross-border procedures will also come into play. We have a 2-year period of intensive work ahead of us on the implementation of the General Data Protection Regulation and the so-called. the police directive. This will require both legislative work, the creation of new rules and procedures, as well as organisational changes and changes in the structure of the GIODO Bureau in order to enable these tasks to be carried out, which will force us to reform the rules. All challenges that bring with them progressive technological, social, economic and legal changes require an increase in the budget of the inspector's general office so that the office can effectively carry out current and new tasks. On behalf of the Parliamentary Club, Law and Justice, I would like to present my position on the report on the activities of the General Data Protection Officer covering two years of his activities, i.e. 2014 and 2015. The General Data Protection Officer has a significant role in the system of national authorities due to his or her responsibilities and competences. It combines powers in the field of civil rights with far-reaching control powers and powers to take decisions of a sovereign nature. There is no doubt that the control exercised by the General Data Protection Officer is extremely important in a democratic state, since it concerns one of the fundamental human rights of the right to privacy. The guarantee of this right is one of the determinants of the proper functioning of a democratic society. The data protection system should be adapted to human needs and ensure that human development is unfettered. Therefore, the protection of the individual's personal rights, its privacy is the primary objective of the general inspector's operation. The consequence of this is the requirement for a regulatory regime which gives it the character of a body specialised in data protection and interpretation of its rules. The relevant powers and responsibilities conferred on the general inspector shall allow the competent and objective performance of his statutory tasks. The system must not only determine the authority's competence but also guarantee the means to ensure its effectiveness. Proper control and effectiveness of the measures taken in the event of irregularities detected ensure a correct and secure process of data creation and processing, thereby guaranteeing the legal interests of the individual. The GIODO activity report was presented at the meeting of the Committee on Justice and Human Rights on 22 June. Compared to 2014 and 2015 there is a clear increase in revenues to registers - about 25.5% More. On behalf of the Civic Platform Parliamentary Club, I would like to present our position on the reports on the activities of the Data Protection Officer General in 2014. Art. 51 The Constitution of the Republic of Poland reads: No one can be obliged otherwise than under the Act to disclose information concerning him or her. Public authorities may not collect, collect and share information about citizens other than those necessary in a democratic legal state. Everyone shall have the right of access to official documents and data sets relating to him. A limitation of that right may be laid down by law. Everyone has the right to seek correction and to remove false, incomplete or otherwise collected information contrary to the law. So much for the constitution. But the right to protection of information was provided as one of civil liberties not only in the Constitution of the Republic of Poland, but also in international conventions and agreements concerning human rights. It has become extremely important just now, in times of universal and rapid Internet access, dynamic development of telecommunications, modern technologies and media. In times where information is one of the most valuable things. Personal data are a special type of information. In order to facilitate the activity and development of services, registers and data of clients of offices, service companies, shops in the vast majority of cases are processed entirely electronically. Unfortunately, if they are not properly secured, there is a risk that dishonest entities will steal them and use them contrary to the objective agreed by the controller and the person who entrusted them. Moreover, more and more individuals on various types of portals, such as social networks, websites and forums make information available from their private lives, not fully aware of what they can be used for, unfortunately not realizing that anonymity on the Internet is an illusion. Therefore, first of all, it is important that each of us protect our personal data on the one hand, only in justified cases. We must be aware that the more information we disseminate, the greater the risk that they can be used unfairly. Secondly, efforts should be made to ensure that there are appropriate provisions for the storage and protection of personal data and that the authorities that monitor their compliance are equipped with appropriate instruments to do so. It's a difficult and extensive task. His image gives us the reports presented by the Minister, by the General Data Protection Officer. I will not discuss all the issues raised in the reports. I'd like to stop by with a few of them that got my attention. The first issue is the operation of the Office General for the Protection of Personal Data. Four and a half thousand. requests for interpretation of legislation and nearly 2,5 thousand. complaints received by GIODO in 2015 - these numbers are impressive and seem to be increasing from year to year. The register of personal data collections maintained by GIODO is also growing dynamically. I am aware, of course, that this would entail specific costs, but it is certainly necessary to consider the financial strengthening of this office. It is also worth considering some systemic changes, which are suggested by the General Data Protection Officer. I am referring, for example, to the issue of a legislative initiative which GIODO does not have, and to the right to request the Constitutional Court to declare that the laws issued by the central state authorities are incompatible with the Constitution of the Republic of Poland, the ratified international agreements and laws. Another point is the attention addressed to us parliamentarians. As the General Inspector points out, not all draft normative acts relevant to the protection of personal data are submitted to him for an opinion. In my opinion, this is also worth changing. Another observation from the reading of the report concerns the necessity of education of Poles on the protection of personal data and the rights they have in this regard. I'm talking about the right to be forgotten. Only some citizens realise that they have the right, under certain conditions, to request the cessation of data processing on their subject and to delete them. I would also like to draw attention to the issue of video monitoring, which is one of the most problematic and regulatory issues, as the General Inspector himself points out. Surveillance cameras, especially in cities, are practically on every corner. In addition to those working under municipal surveillance, they are basically in every store, bank, hospital or office. The main reasons for this are security issues, and in many cases it is difficult to disagree. However, there may be too much interference in our privacy. Unfortunately, as GIODO points out, there are only residual regulations on certain aspects of the monitoring operation. Therefore, appropriate legislative measures should be taken to put an end to this as soon as possible. In conclusion, thank you very much, Minister, for the information presented in the report. ParlaMint-PL_2016-07-07-sejm-22-3.u510	Thank you very much, sir. Mr. Tomasz Jaskoła put the club's position in writing. I would like to ask Mrs Marta Golbik, the Modern Club.
ParlaMint-PL_2016-07-07-sejm-22-3.u511	Mrs. Marshal! High Izbo! With regard to the reports of the General Data Protection Officer in 2014 and 2015, it is worth raising a few additional issues. As far as print 116 is concerned, given that the new rules largely aim to achieve a higher level of enforcement of the obligation for companies to process data, it can be seen that they are part of the amendment of the Easier Business Act of 7 November 2014, so-called. Fourth deregulation bill. Entrepreneurs as data controllers had to decide in a short time on the shape, the form of their compliance with the obligations laid down in the Act and prepare for them in such a way that they were not too expensive. The most changes concern the information security administrator. In a similar way to the ABI project, many large entrepreneurs already operate, and smaller ones tend to find it rare. The new rules will oblige the data controller to choose one of the two options, i.e. either implement and independently supervise data protection, or appoint an information security administrator and report it to the ABI registry maintained by GIODO. Then ABI will be responsible for the implementation of the documentation and the submission of annual reports to the controller of personal data. The responsibilities, but also the rights of the administrator, have therefore been extended. These duties are above all so-called duties. checking the compliance of the processing of personal data with the data protection regulations. The frequency and precise form of carrying out the verification activities shall be determined by an implementing regulation. According to Art. Article 19b (1) (b) of Regulation (EU) No 1306/2013 shall apply. 1 with an order to check by the administrator may request GIODO, indicating the scope and date of the check. The next obligation is to keep a register of data sets processed by the controller, except for those subject to statutory exemption from registration. The registry that the administrator keeps is open and everyone has the right to browse it. The second important change is related to improving the transfer of personal data to third countries outside the European Economic Area - which is very common at present, in a time of globalisation. Before the amendment, the failure to comply with the statutory conditions set out in the Act led to the need to request the relevant consent from GIODO. The amendment confirmed that adequate protection for traders in a third country - the importer - ensures the application of standard contractual clauses approved by the European Commission or binding corporate rules. In the second case, i.e. However, when applying general rules for the processing of personal data binding in a corporation, it is necessary to obtain acceptance of the authority in one country of the European Economic Area. It is envisaged that the data protection authorities of the Member States of the European Economic Area may be consulted. The entrepreneur, setting up the information security administrator, reports this fact to GIODO. He must also file an appeal. The administrator, with the extended competence assigned by the entrepreneur, implements the acts laid down in the Act, so far provided for by GIODO, on its own, e.g. - in demanding cases, self-control. Consequently, these activities must be supplemented by reporting as part of internal documentation. At the same time, it should be hoped that these checks commissioned by GIODO will not be very frequent and that they will be within reasonable ranges and deadlines. The changes made to the information security administrator will certainly benefit the most from those entrepreneurs who have already set it up in a similar form to the one proposed in the project. In particular, in the case of large companies with a strong ABI position, these will be solutions that confirm and support their effective action in this area, such as: the possibility of self-control of the administrator, which is in certain cases an alternative to GIODO control, or e.g. resignation, when there is an information security administrator, from unnecessary recording of data sets. However, it should be borne in mind that this is linked to specific expenditures: on the functioning of the administrator - especially where the obligations arising from the act are fulfilled by the administrator himself - or in connection with the obligation to keep a register of collections in public, when additional expenditure needs to be taken into account, e.g. to maintain a website or purchase software. Also in connection with the not yet fully defined wording of the Union Regulation, it may turn out that the costs incurred in this regard are partly unnecessary, since obligations will be differently shaped. Therefore, it seems that these solutions are less favourable to many smaller companies, where in fact there is no information security administrator similar to the designed one. Part of GIODO's responsibilities have been transferred to entrepreneurs. Entrepreneurs do not receive support from GIODO in case of unclear legal provisions or lack of clear interpretation of legal provisions in previous judgments. For print No 582, p. 291. A timely commitment has been made to notify the controller of the information or personal data sets. GIODO's deadline is not guaranteed. The registrations last up to a few months. ParlaMint-PL_2016-07-07-sejm-22-3.u522	Mr. Marshal! High Izbo! On behalf of the Parliamentary Club of the Polish People's Party, I will present my position on the reports on the activities of the General Data Protection Officer in 2014 and 2015, prints 116 and 582, together with the position of the committee, print No 619. The report shall be the implementation of the Article. 20 of the Personal Data Protection Act, according to which the General Data Protection Officer submits to the Sejm once a year a report on his or her activities, together with conclusions resulting from compliance with the provisions on personal data protection. In 2015, the Polish Parliament appointed Dr Edyta Bielak-Jomaa as General Data Protection Officer. During the reporting period there was a very significant change in the legal framework for the protection of personal data in Poland. The Act on the facilitation of economic activity entered into force on 1 January 2015. The changes concerned the functioning of the information security administrator and the exemption from the requirement to obtain the consent of GIODO to transfer personal data to a third country which does not ensure an adequate level of protection of personal data in its territory when they are transferred on the basis of standard contractual clauses or binding corporate rules. The new rules introduce an institution of binding corporate rules in the Polish legal order and define the procedure for their approval by GIODO. The solutions adopted, regardless of their deregulation objective, reduced the number of responsibilities of the data controllers who appointed ABI, e.g. the obligation to report personal data sets to GIODO in this case has been removed - also aimed at preparing data controllers for the application of the General Data Protection Regulation as part of a package of reforms planned in the EU in the area of personal data protection. The tasks and powers of the General Data Protection Officer shall be governed by the provisions of the above-mentioned Rules. Act of 29 August 1997 on the protection of personal data. In their light, GIODO shall be entitled to: - checking the compliance of data processing with personal data protection rules, - issuing administrative decisions and handling complaints concerning the implementation of personal data protection rules, - ensure the performance by the obliged persons of a non-monetary nature resulting from decisions taken by the application of enforcement measures provided for in the Law of 17 June 1966 on enforcement proceedings in the administration (Journal of Laws No. U. z 2014 poz. 1619, late. zm.), - keeping a register of data sets and a register of information security administrators, as well as providing information on registered data sets and registered information security administrators, - the opinion of draft laws and regulations on the protection of personal data, - initiating and undertaking personal data protection projects, - participate in the work of international organisations and institutions dealing with the protection of personal data. The Parliamentary Club of the Polish People's Party is in favour of the report.

First of all, I would like to point out that, on the basis of data from the Central Statistical Office, the rate of consumption in Poland has remained slightly above 9l of 100 percent alcohol per capita since 2007. In 2014, its consumption decreased by 0.27l per person compared to 2013 to 9,4l. In terms of the structure of alcohol consumption, the trend has changed by increasing the volume of beer consumed - almost 60% - at the expense of the volume of alcohol consumed - around 34% - where there was a 3% drop. On the other hand, the share of wine has increased for the first time since 1998 to 8% in 2014. It should be noted that the amount of excise duty is an important instrument for regulating the level of alcohol consumption in the country. Over a period of several years, it can be observed that following a sharp increase in consumption, which occurred in 2003, following a reduction in excise duty on ethyl alcohol, there has been an increase in excise duty on all types of alcohol since 2009 - there has been a slowdown in the upward trend. Thus, in terms of reducing the economic availability of alcohol in 2014 as a result of an increase of 15% excise duty on ethyl alcohol has resulted in a price increase of 2.6% in price availability of this type of alcohol. In 2014, there was also a reduction in sales of alcoholic beverages (there were 273 people per sale point) compared to the previous years, i.e. 266 people in 2013 and 246 in 2009. This study showed total abstinence of Poles studied in the last 12 months at the level of approximately 28%. Compared with the results of the previous study in 2009, the proportion of adults drinking alcoholic beverages decreased by more than 2 percentage points. The results of the study confirmed the denial of vodka and other spirit drinks by beer, which accounts for 70% total consumption of pure alcohol and less than 20% of alcohol and other spirits. Against the background of these two dominant alcoholic beverages, the share of wine is modest and slightly above 10%. Total abstinence in the last 12 months declared close to 28% test subjects. The largest group of people who drink alcohol are 30 and 49 years of age. Women are much less likely than men to declare frequent drinking, although these disparities are gradually decreasing. The proportion of women drinking alcohol decreased from about 66% in 2009 to 63% in 2014 Among men, it also fell from 85% to 83%. At least once a week, one out of three drinking men admitted to drinking alcohol, but only one in seven drinking women. According to the results of the study, a statistical man drinking alcohol a week drinks about four bottles of 0.5 l beer and a little more than 80 ml vodka or other spirit drinks, while a statistical woman drinking a week drinks about 1.5 bottles of 0.5 l beer and two glasses of wine. The level of alcohol consumption of children and young people based on the results of the study on health and health behaviour of schoolchildren against selected demographic conditions, carried out by the Institute of Mother and Child in 2015 at least once during its life, was drinking alcohol 42.6% students aged 11 to 15, of which 6.4% at least once a week. One in five teens were found to have been drunk at least once in their lives - 21% , and 3.2% It's done more than ten times. Although these data are worrying compared to previous studies, we are seeing a significant decrease in overall drinking rates as well as in drinking by young people in particular in this age group, 11 and 15 years of age, and alcohol initiation It's happening more and more later. On the other hand, positive changes are expressed by an increasingly widespread abstinence attitude among young people. The family plays a very important role in the social context of alcoholism. Therefore, multilateral action is an important recommendation for all those involved in the alcohol policy of the institutions and bodies, as public health should not only be an area of interest to the Minister of Health. It should be pointed out that prevention should take place more and more places in reducing alcohol consumption. Strategies aimed at the context of drinking are more effective if they are part of a preventive programme. This trend may be linked to an increase in the price of high-interest drinks. This leads to a slight, but always, reduction in the availability of physical alcohol, which experts believe is a trend that has been going in the desired direction for several years. It is also worth noting the trend of a decreasing number of accidents involving persons who are intoxicated. An important factor in reducing road drunkenness is the progressive increase in police controls and the taking of information and education activities, for example. Stop the drunk driver. Over 15 million sobriety studies were carried out in 2014. Local authorities have also done a lot of work. In order to increase the availability and quality of assistance to victims of violence in families with alcohol problems affecting adults and children, local government units continued their efforts in 2014 to maintain support infrastructure for those suffering from violence in the family. According to the report, local authorities are spending more and more resources according to the provisions of the Act. For 2013, the total expenditure increased by 4,7%. , mainly due to the larger expenditure of local governments (12.8% higher than in 2013) and municipalities (4.4% higher) ). Of course, such a child requires constant care, usually two parents, because it is difficult to take care of one of the parents almost 24 hours a day. They were murdered in front of the 11-year-old brother of little Halinka using tools such as axes, hammers, maces, pitchforks, scythes, cocks. Their property was stolen and the buildings burned down. On that tragic day, both my mom and I went to visit their sick grandmother, and that's how they survived. To this day, in the place of Wola Ostrowiecka there are collective farm fields. At that time the lives of the inhabitants of the Eastern Kresów II of the Republic were vibrant. Daddy Halinki led the breeding of horses for ulans from the Polish army. They also left with his soul. Sometimes you can find rusty horseshoes here with a little luck. Halinka's traumatic history is one case, and there were tens of thousands of them.
